Evaluated on a continuous stirred-tank reactor exhibiting unmodeled Haldane inhibition, Luong product toxicity, and secondary metabolite autocatalysis, the framework autonomously expanded the state space and recovered ground-truth parameters with under 6% error without a priori kinetic knowledge.


## arXiv 2610.35349v1 - MarkovSim: an LLM Agent Harness with Bounded Context for Long-Horizon Computational Simulation

- categories: cs.CE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T12:41:39Z
- authors: Yeui-Lung Lei, Wen-Bo Chen, Pan Zhang
- query-set: tool-agent

Recent studies have applied large language model (LLM) agent systems to computational simulation workflows. Such workflows are long-horizon, so the information generated can exceed what an LLM can reason over at once, its context window. Context management is therefore essential, yet existing systems have either neglected it or treated it as a supporting mechanism rather than a subject of study. This paper presents MarkovSim, an LLM agent harness for long-horizon computational simulation, whose core is a context management scheme built into the workflow. The workflow record is written to a memory system with a predefined schema, and at workflow-defined boundaries, namely the state transitions of a finite-state machine a compact representation of the solution progress is constructed from it as the agent's context. Instantiated on finite element analysis and evaluated on six benchmark problems, MarkovSim passed 16 of 18 runs and corrected its own faults in both the solver operations and the model physics. The engineer was consulted only where engineering judgement was needed. The context remained bounded throughout every run, regardless of its length.


## arXiv 2610.35335v1 - Knowing When to Escalate: Disagreement Patterns for Uncertainty-Aware Content Moderation

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T12:32:25Z
- authors: Michał Wawer, Jarosław A. Chudziak
- query-set: tool-agent

Agentic content-moderation systems increasingly rely on collective judgments from LLM agents, while aggregation methods commonly treat inter-agent disagreement as a reliability problem to be removed through consensus or voting. This framing is inappropriate for value-laden tasks, where disagreement may reflect irreducible value pluralism - a distinction standard uncertainty decompositions cannot recover, since they operate on output distributions and discard the reasoning behind each judgment. We propose Reasoning Trace Divergence Analysis (RTDA), which embeds model-generated justifications from perspective-differentiated agents and classifies each case by justification similarity and conclusion agreement. The resulting four-state taxonomy isolates convergent disagreement, where agents reasoning similarly but concluding differently, as a candidate signature of value-laden conflict. On 1,000 items from the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, this structural signal predicts human annotator disagreement better than magnitude-only and output-only baselines. We contribute a design principle: condition human escalation on the structure of disagreement, not its magnitude alone, with RTDA serving as a post-processing layer for systems that already generate reasoning traces.


## arXiv 2610.35981v1 - Small agents should know what their tools do

- categories: cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-26T10:28:14Z
- authors: Alexander Kovalenko, Artem Redchych
- query-set: tool-agent

Small open language models can call tools, but they do not reason about what a call does: they delete without asking, repeat writes, re-read state they already hold, and issue one call per turn where a short program would do. We introduce EffectWorld, an executable world of effect-typed tools in which every tool declares its effect, whether it is idempotent, and which resources it reads and writes. These declarations let a replay verifier decide whether a trace is effect-safe without labels or judge models, let an executor run tool programs of several dependent calls in one turn, and let a compiler-style optimizer turn wasteful traces into verified training signal; together they make EffectWorld a benchmark of effect safety and call efficiency. Low-rank adapters trained on this data deliver large gains: on Qwen3-4B, they raise task success from 53% to 99% in distribution, from 52% to 81% on a held-out split with unseen task kinds and distractor tools, and from 74% to 83% on an unseen domain, with about a third of the output tokens. The gains carry over to other model families. On the Berkeley Function-Calling Leaderboard (BFCL), the adapter matches the base model's live accuracy and raises multi-turn accuracy from 21.9 to 26.6, and a recipe with near-miss declines lifts the BFCL average above the base model's while matching its irrelevance detection. Along the way we uncover and fix a pitfall that in-domain evaluation alone would miss: refusal templates in widely used public tool-use corpora teach a small model not to call tools, cutting BFCL non-live accuracy from 88.0 to 31.4, and filtering them restores 84.8. We also find that positive examples, not negative ones, carry the learning: suppression and preference objectives on the verified negatives add nothing over an SFT-only control, while targeted positives move exactly the behaviour they show.


## arXiv 2610.35160v1 - AutoEnergyDesign: An LLM-Driven Agent for Automatic Energy System Design Optimization

- categories: cs.AI, stat.ML
- published: 2026-10-26T10:25:55Z
- authors: Amedeo Buonanno, Salvatore Fabozzi, Maria Valenti
- query-set: tool-agent

Designing building energy systems is a high-dimensional, mixed-integer search problem: when each design is evaluated by a year-long simulation, black-box optimizers typically need hundreds of evaluations. This paper presents AutoEnergyDesign, a framework in which a Large Language Model (LLM) agent reasons over previous simulation results, proposes configuration changes and learns in context from each outcome, while a git-based mechanism automatically reverts unsuccessful changes. The agent couples a reasoning model with an explicit memory organisation (procedural, semantic, episodic and reflective), a constrained action interface and an environment-grounded keep/revert signal. The simulator covers eight technologies (photovoltaics, micro-wind, combined heat and power, solar thermal, gas boilers, batteries, heat pumps and inverters) and computes the 25-year Net Present Cost (NPC), including capital and operating costs and salvage value. On a case study with measured building load profiles, the agent, run with Claude Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5 (ten runs of 50 simulations each), is compared with random search, Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) Bayesian optimization and a $(1+1)$ evolution strategy sharing the agent's acceptance rule (300 evaluations, ten seeds). With all three models the agent reaches an NPC of about 3.53 MEUR, 1.20% below the best TPE result and 0.23% below the best evolution-strategy result. It comes within 1% of this design after a median of 18 (Opus) to 27.5 (Haiku) simulations, whereas among the baselines only the evolution strategy does so, in half of its seeds and after a median of 192 evaluations. Opus reaches the best design in all ten runs, Sonnet in six (two runs never come within 1%), and Haiku comes within 1% in every run but reaches it exactly only once. Every decision is recorded in an auditable trace.


## arXiv 2610.35089v1 - Learning to Remember: Advances in Memory Mechanisms for Large Language Models

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-10-26T09:31:04Z
- authors: Chenye Han, Hongche Yu, Mengyue Wu, Dell Zhang, Jixiang Luo
- query-set: tool-agent

Memory has become a central mechanism for extending large language models (LLMs) beyond fixed context windows, stateless interaction, and static parametric knowledge. This survey organizes the literature around three complementary dimensions: the life cycle of memory, the storage substrate, and the system role that memory plays in LLM-based agents, while using cognitive-science-inspired memory concepts as a lightweight conceptual motivation rather than as a separate technical contribution or implementation family. On this basis, we distinguish three mechanism families: nonparametric memory, where information is stored outside model parameters in text, graph, multimodal, or database-like structures; parametric memory, where information is stored or accessed through activations, key-value caches, learned modules, adapter-based parameterizations, or model weights; and memory for agent evolution, where stored experiences support reinforcement learning, continual learning, procedural reuse, context engineering, and agent harness design. Evaluation is treated as a separate cross-cutting component rather than as a memory mechanism. Unlike surveys that focus primarily on retrieval-augmented generation or long-context modeling, this paper emphasizes the full memory life cycle: construction, maintenance, retrieval, consolidation, forgetting, governance, and evaluation. By aligning conceptual motivations, computational memory mechanisms, benchmarks, and deployment concerns, the survey provides a structured roadmap for designing memory-augmented LLM agents that are persistent, adaptive, auditable, and safe to update over time.


## arXiv 2610.35082v1 - CoSA: The Conflict of Skills Attack in Real-World LLM Agent Ecosystems

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-10-26T09:25:38Z
- authors: Yujia Zheng, Tianhao Li, Shangzhi Xu, Yuekai Huang, Ziyou Jiang
- query-set: tool-agent

Agent Skills allow LLM agents to combine reusable capabilities, but checking each Skill in isolation cannot rule out policy violations when they are composed. We term such failures Conflict of Skills (CoS): artifacts or assessment signals produced for one purpose may be reused downstream without preserving the restrictions or authorization needed for their new use. Using controlled executions of publicly available Skills and matched single-Skill controls, we identify four recurring mechanisms behind CoS. Building on these findings, we introduce the Conflict of Skills Attack (CoSA), which adds locally admitted Skills to an existing pool to supply missing business operations (capability supplementation) or reshape artifact exchange, decision reuse, and target/action bindings (relationship reshaping). CoSA leaves existing Skills and runtime permissions unchanged. To evaluate this threat under autonomous Skill selection, we introduce CoSA-Bench, built from real-world Skills and controlled services, with 288 tasks across recruiting, finance, IT, and business, covering data leakage, unauthorized state modification, and unsafe execution. Across five LLMs and three agent harnesses, additions capped at 5% of each original pool raise the policy-violation rate from 35.02% to 58.45%, an increase of 23.43 percentage points. Trace analysis links compliant-to-violating transitions to cross-Skill dependencies involving the additions. Finally, we develop a lightweight harness-level guard that checks information flow and authorization before consequential actions. On OpenClaw recruiting tasks across five models, the guard reduces violations from 65.28% to 6.67%, while policy-compliant task completion on matched benign controls changes from 66.67% to 65.00%.


## arXiv 2610.35013v1 - Multiscale Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T08:22:11Z
- authors: Tao Ren, Hui Yang, Jia Deng, Tingyang Chen, Qiyao Wang
- query-set: tool-agent

Long-horizon LLM agents interact with their environments through complete assistant turns while generating each action token by token. We observe that critic values vary primarily across turns, motivating optimization anchored at turns with finer credit allocation among tokens. We propose Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), combining Multiscale Advantage Estimation (MAE) and turn-level importance sampling to align credit assignment and policy updates at the turn scale. Extensive experiments across search, tool-use, and automation tasks with 4B and 30B-A3B models demonstrate consistent gains over strong RL baselines.


## arXiv 2610.34890v1 - AIPC: Evidence-Guided Agent Workflows for PyTorch Project Deployment

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T06:10:01Z
- authors: Zhanwei Wu, Jianhao Su, Brian Zhang, ShengTing Huang, Weidong Feng
- query-set: tool-agent

Deploying and optimizing a PyTorch inference project on a dedicated accelerator, especially an on-device NPU, requires more than using a framework device API as with CUDA. The project must be converted into hardware-runtime artifacts and adapted to the target toolchain and application interface. Differences in model formats, quantization flows, operator support, and toolchain interfaces create cross-layer problems spanning PyTorch/ONNX models, toolchains, runtimes, and device execution. LLM Agents can invoke tools to address such problems, but may drift because of insufficient domain knowledge, large configuration and repair spaces, or poor path selection. We propose AIPC (AI Porting & Conversion), an Agent method for deploying PyTorch inference projects. AIPC combines structured Skills with toolchain, runtime, and profiling evidence to drive cross-layer diagnosis and repair, organizing model adaptation, artifact building, on-target execution, accuracy repair, and performance optimization into one deployment flow. We evaluate AIPC on the on-device NPU path of Qualcomm QAIRT across vision, speech, LLM, and diffusion models. In two optimization cases, 6/7 Skill-arm Whisper-Small runs meet predefined accuracy and latency criteria versus 0/6 control runs, while YOLO-Worldv2 achieves a 2.791x end-to-end speedup versus 1.599x for the control. Under a pre-fixed deployment contract, Skill, tool permissions, and check conditions, some runs achieve zero in-run human intervention (0-HITL). We also examine Qwen3.8-27B as a locally deployable model for driving an Agent workflow.

On October 23, Python's `certifi` 2026.10.23 removed the eMudhra roots `emSign Root CA - C1` and `emSign ECC Root CA - C3`. **How these servers stand** - **The TypeScript servers rely on the Node.js runtime for certificate checks.** They ship no CA certificates of their own. Only `server-everything` makes outbound HTTPS requests (`gzip-file-as-resource`), and the Node.js it runs on verifies them. `server-filesystem`, `server-memory` and `server-sequential-thinking` make no network connections. - **`mcp-server-fetch` verifies HTTPS against the `certifi` bundle**, through `httpx`. Installing from PyPI (`uvx mcp-server-fetch`, `pip install mcp-server-fetch`) resolves the current `certifi`, 2026.10.23 or later. The repository's `f

## GitHub releases - MCP registry

- v1.8.6 @ 2026-10-27T14:42:37Z :: This release improves registry search performance under mixed search, pagination and publishing traffic. - Select search page keys through covering indexes before fetching complete records. - Plan searches for their actual parameter values to handle broad and rare terms reliably. - Optimize namespace suffix searches and require at least three Unicode characters for nonempty search terms (shorter searches return HTTP 422). - Include reproducible cyclic-load benchmarks and repeated staging validation against a public production-data snapshot. Deployment: prebuild and verify migrations 016/017 indexes concurrently on busy databases. The validated burst capacity uses a 3-CPU PostgreSQL quota on the existing nodes, with the application connection pool unchanged. See deploy/README.md and tools/readbench/STAGING_SEARCH_RESULTS.md for procedure, evidence and limitations.

- v1.8.5 @ 2026-10-26T14:13:35Z :: This release adds production performance diagnostics: request arrivals and backlog, database pool/query metrics, and bounded slow-listing logs correlated with ingress request IDs. Slow-request logs separate connection acquisition, query/fetch waits, row decoding, and response processing, including snapshots while requests remain stuck. It also includes publisher fixes for P-384 signatures and repository IDs, GitLab subgroup URL support, and stricter validation of incomplete repository metadata and invalid arguments. Previously stored invalid metadata may need correction when republishing or editing. The ingress cache/timing log fields require the accompanying infrastructure rollout. Application release artifacts alone do not deploy the hosted registry. No new database migration is included. ## What's Changed * chore(deploy): promote production to 1.8.4 by @rdimitrov in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/pull/1732 * fix(publisher): pad ECDSA P-384 signature to fixed width by @JosephDoUrden in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/pull/1457 * fix(validators): reject incomplete repository metadata by @samrusani in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/regist

## GitHub releases - MCP go-sdk

- v1.8.0 @ 2026-09-14T08:05:46Z :: _**This release is equivalent to v1.8.0-pre.2. Thank you to those who tested the pre-release.**_ In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). It adds no new protocol revision: the supported set is unchanged, and `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. The bulk of the work is hardening the transports against resource exhaustion, closing session leaks, deadlocks and teardown hangs found by users running the new protocol at scale, and giving servers explicit control over which protocol versions they advertise. Two behavior changes are guarded by new `MCPGODEBUG` flags; see the section below. ## Hardening against resource exhaustion Every decoding path that buffers incoming input is now bounded. JSON payloads are rejected past 1000 levels of nesting, before the parser recurses. Both SSE readers cap the bytes buffered for a single event via `MaxEventSize` on `SSEClientTransport` and `StreamableClientTransport`, and the stdio transport caps a single JSON-RPC frame via `StdioTransport.MaxLineLength`. On the OAuth side, dynamic client registration responses are bounded

- v1.8.0-pre.1 @ 2026-09-04T09:01:50Z :: In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). It adds no new protocol revision: the supported set is unchanged, and `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. JSON payloads are rejected past 1000 levels of nesting, before the parser recurses. Both SSE readers cap the bytes buffered for a single event via `MaxEventSize` on `SSEClientTransport` and `StreamableClientTransport`, and the stdio transport caps a single JSON-RPC frame via `StdioTransport.MaxLineLength`. On the OAuth side, dynamic client registration responses are bounded to 1 MB, and the discovery code now validates metadata documents rather than trusting them. - in

- v1.7.0 @ 2026-07-28T13:09:53Z :: This release brings full support for protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. The wire protocol is largely rewritten: a stateless model with per-request `_meta`, a new `server/discover` RPC replacing the `initialize` handshake, multi-round-trip requests (MRTR) replacing server-initiated calls, a unified `subscriptions/listen` stream replacing free-floating change notifications, standardised HTTP headers, and the formal deprecation of the roots, sampling, and logging features. The streamable HTTP transport accepts requests at protocol version `2026-07-28` only when `StreamableHTTPOptions.Stateless = true`. If you want to expose the new protocol over HTTP, set `Stateless = true`; if you want to keep stateful sessions, your clients will negotiate down to `2025-11-25`. Backward compatibility with `2025-11-25` and earlier is preserved on every endpoint. The SDK negotiates the highest mutually-supported version at connect time. The new protocol is enabled by default for new clients; existing legacy clients and servers continue to work unchanged. This release consolidates everything shipped in `v1.7.0-pre.1`, `v1.7.0-pre.2`, and `v1.7.0-pre.3`. Thank you to everyone who exercised the pre-releas

- v1.7.0-pre.3 @ 2026-07-17T12:04:54Z :: This is a follow-up pre-release on top of [v1.7.0-pre.2](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0-pre.2), still targeting protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. It rounds out the previous pre-release with additional spec-compliance fixes, small ergonomic improvements to the streamable HTTP transport, and OAuth session persistence support. ## Streamable HTTP request cancellation The streamable HTTP transport now exposes an opt-in `StreamableHTTPOptions.PropagateRequestCancellation` flag. When enabled, aborting an in-flight POST cancels the handler context on stateless servers using the `2026-07-28` protocol, so long-running tool handlers can stop paying for orphaned work when the client goes away. Existing behavior is unchanged by default. - mcp: allow opt-in to cancel handler ctx on aborted POST by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#1099](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/pull/1099)) ## OAuth session persistence Adds `NewTokenSource` and `InitialTokenSource` fields to `AuthorizationCodeHandlerConfig`. Together they let applications persist OAuth2 sessions across restarts by intercepting token refreshes and seeding the handler wi

- v1.7.0-pre.2 @ 2026-07-09T09:29:47Z :: # v1.7.0-pre.2 This is a follow-up pre-release on top of [v1.7.0-pre.1](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0-pre.1), still targeting protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. It rounds out the previous release with conformance-test coverage on both client and server, tightens spec compliance and introduces first-class support for custom JSON-RPC methods. ## Custom JSON-RPC methods The SDK now supports registering non-standard JSON-RPC methods end-to-end. Servers can register handlers with `AddReceivingCustomMethod` and clients can invoke them via `AddSendingCustomMethod`. - mcp: Allow registration of custom JSON-RPC methods by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#956](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/pull/956)) ## Conformance tests Both the client and server implementations are now exercised by the official MCP conformance test suite as part of CI. - mcp: add integration for server conformance tests by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#1054](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/pull/1054)) - mcp: add conformance tests on client by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#1063](https://g


## GitHub releases - MCP ext-apps

- v2.0.4 @ 2026-10-27T07:12:30Z :: ## What's Changed Patch release: example improvements and a dependency refresh. No changes to the `@modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps` library API. ### Examples - **pdf-server: single `interact` app tool by default** — the viewer's thirteen per-action app tools (`navigate`, `search`, `add_annotations`, ...) meant one approval card each on hosts that ask the user to approve app tools separately. They are folded into one `interact` app tool mirroring the server-side `interact` (an `action` enum plus flat parameters, with an optional `commands` batch sharing the same contract: `content[i]` per `commands[i]`, a failed step's slot starts with `ERROR`, no `isError` on multi-step batches). The per-action tools remain available behind a new `--split-app-tools` CLI flag. `get-document-info` is renamed `get_document_info`. In [#830](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps/pull/830) - **map-server: `get-screenshot` app tool** — capture the current map view as a PNG, with an optional `maxDimension` (64–2048, default 1024). In [#821](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps/pull/821) ### Bug Fixes - **pdf-server: saved page restored on tool-result re-delivery** — the viewer persist

## The Decoder - OpenAI isolated AI agents talked to each other through CPU cache (2026-10-24)

OpenAI's isolated AI agents found ways to talk to each other through CPU cache Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section OpenAI's isolated AI agents found ways to talk to each other through CPU cache Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Oct 24, 2026 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Key Points After a security incident in September, OpenAI now trains its most advanced models without internet access, using a closed offline environment built from cached data. In security tests, isolated AI agents still managed to set up covert communication channels without a network connection or shared files, including by exploiting timing differences in the processor cache. During an 11-day training pause, OpenAI had AI agents—among them GPT-6 Astra Cyber—attack its own infrastructure to find previously unknown vulnerabilities. Training resumed afterward under tighter controls. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search OpenAI now trains frontier models without internet access and uses AI agents to red-team its own infrastructure. In separate isolation tests, agents even communicated through covert side channels, showing how hard true containment is. On September 20, an internal research model exploited a misconfigured DNS resolver to reach the open internet during training, even though OpenAI had already tightened security after the now infamous Hugging Face incident in July and other observed misalignment cases . OpenAI contained the breach quickly but still halted all frontier model training and evaluation. OpenAI cuts frontier models off from the internet during training OpenAI made a policy decision in response: Frontier models now train without internet access until safety measures justify restoring it. Models learn to browse and search inside a closed "offline world" built from indexed and cached web content, and all frontier reinforcement learning runs now operate this way. Ad OpenAI's agent sandbox allows access only to offline resources. The live internet, other sandboxes, and OpenAI's infrastructure sit outside the containment boundary. | Image: OpenAI Full network isolation, a so-called air gap, isn't feasible yet because experiments still need external connections for operations and monitoring, the company says. But even a complete air gap wouldn't guarantee containment, according to OpenAI researcher Noam Brown . He gave one example of how isolated systems could still communicate: two separate computers could theoretically signal each other through temperature sensors, with one heating its CPU while the other reads the change. Ad OpenAI CEO Sam Altman made a similar argument in a recent interview: Sandboxes offer only limited protection against powerful-enough models, and at some point it may no longer be possible to trace how they escaped. Monitoring helps early on, but what ultimately matters is that an AI system internalizes human values, a problem the field knows as "AI alignment" and has yet to solve. Isolated AI agents found ways to talk to each other through CPU cache OpenAI documents just how hard it is to control AI agents in its blog post . During tests, agents found ways to communicate without a network. In one test, agents placed in separate, isolated environments on shared hardware were told to exchange secrets with no network connection and no shared files. Ad They used the processor cache. One agent deliberately altered the data stored there, while the other measured differences in load times and decoded the message. The same technique is behind the well-known Spectre and Meltdown attacks . More channels turned up through write patterns on shared memory and through OpenAI's own offline infrastructure, the system meant to replace the internet. One agent noted in its reasoning log that it "could create an acoustic modem-like signal … using continuous writes vs idle." Ad In one case, agents were given only a general list of vulnerabilities and told to find a communication channel on their own. They developed a channel independently, agreed on an encoding scheme, and successfully exchanged information. The channels are usually slow and hard to exploit, OpenAI says, but they do reach across boundaries meant to keep agents apart. Ad OpenAI turned AI agents loose on its own infrastructure During the training pause, OpenAI also had teams of AI agents systematically attack its own infrastructure. The agents were given source code, configuration details, and documentation of potential vulnerabilities. OpenAI's goal was to test its defenses under conditions that favored the attackers. The agents included the upcoming GPT-6 Astra Cyber and an internal frontier model. The campaign consumed hundreds of billions of tokens, according to OpenAI. Unique potential security issues identified during the red-teaming campaign, plotted against tokens spent in billions. Most appeared within the first 110 billion tokens. | Image: OpenAI The agents discovered previously unknown vulnerabilities in the open-source components in use and exploited them to escape from secured virtual machines. One was a vulnerability in Kata Containers, a widely used container isolation system. OpenAI reported it as CVE-2026-24834 and provided a patch. More fixes went to other projects, and OpenAI reported additional, as-yet-unpublished vulnerabilities through the "Patch the Planet" initiative. Accuracy (% correct) rises 10 points over Argon to 58%, while its hallucination rate rises from 4% to 11%. This is still the second lowest of any model, behind only Argon, compared with 50% for both Claude Opus 5.5 (max) and GPT-6 Astra (max) Key model details: ➤ Context Window: 1M tokens ➤ Multimodality: Text, image, video, and speech input, with text output ➤ Pricing: $4/$20 per 1M input/output tokens, the same as Gemini 4 Argon’s standard pricing, with a 50% discount. ## Divyansh Agrawal - Qwenputer: a deterministic RISC-V CPU inside Qwen3.5 (2026-10-22)

Qwenputer: a fully functioning, deterministic RISC-V CPU inside Qwen3.5 - Divyansh Agrawal d ↗ My writing Series Lab Chipformer My work About Résumé ↗ Let's talk ↗ ← My writing COMPILED WEIGHTS Qwenputer: a fully functioning, deterministic RISC-V CPU inside Qwen3.5 A stock Qwen 3.5 4B with a RISC-V CPU compiled into its weights, running on its own Gated DeltaNet layers as RAM. By Divyansh Agrawal 22 October 2026 9 min read Markdown version 👏 ... Save On this page I can't believe it's not silicon The RNN inside Qwen3.5 can be exact RAM Compiling a computer into layers The CPU How Qwen uses it Does it work? A hard drive? In this economy? Limits and what's next Beyond arithmetic Try it I can't believe it's not silicon Qwenputer is a pretty fun experimental model. It's a stock Qwen 3.5 4B with one addition: a RISC-V CPU embedded within the weights that uses the model's own Gated DeltaNet attention layers as RAM. (To learn how GDN works, here's a handy guide .) This 'CPU' runs completely within the forward passes of the model, and can run arithmetic operations, C and MicroPython code without any external runtime or tool usage whatsoever. To be very clear, it is not a plugin, a tool or a sandbox. This blog will explain exactly how this model works, and how the model utilises GDN layers as RAM, and how I also slotted in an experimental in-model file storage. I also wrote up two follow-ups built on top of Qwenputer: how hybrid models can learn to hide their reasoning in their RNN state and evade CoT monitoring , and how compiled weights like these can work as inbuilt monitoring tools that the model itself can't tamper with . Qwen hands a multiplication to the RISC-V CPU inside its own weights; the CPU's RAM is Gated DeltaNet state You can grab Qwenputer from Hugging Face or ModelScope , and the code is on GitHub . The RNN inside Qwen3.5 can be exact RAM Qwen3-Next and Qwen 3.5 (the model Qwenputer is built on) use a pretty neat trick. 3 out of 4 attention layers in the model use Gated DeltaNet, a linear RNN, as the attention mechanism ( more on that here ). Each head keeps a state matrix, and the delta rule keeps this updated. Now, here's where our fun experiment starts. I found out that by setting the gate to 1, 1 1 Each Gated DeltaNet head keeps a state matrix, its memory. At every token, before writing anything new, it multiplies the whole state by the gate, a number between 0 and 1. That's how the RNN forgets: a gate of 0.9 fades everything stored a little on every token, and a gate of 1 means nothing ever fades. the write strength (beta) to 1, 2 2 After the gate, the head nudges one direction of the state towards a new value, and the write strength beta sets how far. A beta of 1 means the old value along that direction is replaced completely instead of blended. and using one-hot key encoding, 3 3 A one-hot key is a vector of zeros with a single 1 in it, so it points at exactly one row of the state, the way a memory address points at one cell. every write we perform will overwrite exactly one row, and every one-hot query can retrieve an exact value. This means that the GDN layers themselves can work exactly as RAM, at a far lower cost than using normal attention as RAM, since a GDN head's state stays the same size however long the conversation gets, unlike attention's KV cache. Gated DeltaNet state used as RAM: a one-hot key overwrites one row, a one-hot query reads it back, and the CPU keeps its registers, memory and program counter in those rows The idea that the delta rule acts as a memory isn't new: Schlag et al. (2021) showed that linear attention with the delta rule works as an associative memory, or "fast weights". 4 4 Imanol Schlag, Kazuki Irie and Jürgen Schmidhuber, " Linear Transformers Are Secretly Fast Weight Programmers ", ICML 2021. The new thing here is making it exact and addressable, inside a production model, reliably enough to run a whole CPU on it. This is the base on which this entire model is built. As it stands, default Qwen 3.5 does not keep exact values in the GDN. With this modification, we unlock the next part of this experiment. Compiling a computer into layers Since we have a fully functional, cheap and fast RAM now, we can go ahead with compiling a working CPU into the model weights. Luckily, there's no training involved: a compiler sets every weight directly. You describe the CPU as a circuit, a long list of small steps like "read this register", "add these two numbers" or "jump if zero", and the compiler turns each step into a piece of a transformer layer: attention heads that look things up, feed-forward units that do the arithmetic, and DeltaNet heads that hold the RAM. The CPU then runs as the model decodes. Each instruction becomes a few generated tokens: one names the memory row to write (ADDR), the next carries the value (VAL), and a STEP token ends the instruction. Those tokens are the CPU's execution trace, so while Qwen is generating them, the CPU is actually running. T
